When you play on it, those pellets get into your cleats, your socks, even your car. I'd be vacuuming my floor mats weeks later and still finding them. Plus, when the sun beats down on that fake grass in the summer, it literally radiates heat. It feels like you're running on a grill. We had this one referee, an older guy named Steve, who would always wear these thick black knee-high socks even when it was 90 degrees out. I don't know how he didn't pass out. He was also completely blind to offsides calls. Like, guys would be cherry-picking ten yards behind the defense and he wouldn't blow the whistle. It caused so many fights on the field. \n\nBut yeah, I don't go to that anymore. I don't go to salsa nights either, or even family gatherings really. I just can't deal with people looking at me, and I definitely don't want anyone taking pictures of me. So I just stay home mostly.\n\nDoctor: When you're in social situations, do you feel extremely anxious or self-conscious even when you're not thinking about your face?\n\nPatient: No, not really. If I'm not thinking about my face—which is almost never, honestly—but if I'm somehow distracted, I don't feel anxious just being around people. It's really just the face thing. \n\nLike, back before this started bothering me so much, I was completely fine. I used to go to this diner after work with a couple of the guys from the warehouse. It's this place called Mel's on Route 9, one of those classic chrome-and-neon joints that looks like a bowling alley from the outside. They have these booths covered in that sparkly red vinyl, and it's always sticky, no matter how many times you see the busboy wipe it down with his gray rag. I used to always get their open-faced turkey sandwich. They drown it in this yellow gravy that definitely comes out of a can, but it hits the spot after an eight-hour shift. The waitress there, Barb, she's worked there for probably thirty years. She calls everyone \"hon\" and always has a pencil tucked behind her ear, but the eraser is completely worn down to the metal part. I don't know why she even keeps it if she can't erase anything. You'd think a diner would just buy a box of new pencils, they're like two bucks at Staples. \n\nBut yeah, I never used to get anxious just talking to Barb or hanging out with the guys. It's only now, because I know they can see how messed up the left side of my face is. If it wasn't for that, I'd be totally fine.\n\nDoctor: Do you believe that the issue with the left side of your face is something that others would consider very noticeable or ugly, even though you might not think it's a big deal?\n\nPatient: No, I definitely think it's a huge deal, and I know everyone else sees it too. It's not just some minor thing I'm making up, it's right there on my face. It's so obvious. \n\nLike, when I'm at the grocery store, I can see people staring. I usually go to the Stop & Shop over on Elm, the one with the huge parking lot that always has those rogue shopping carts just rolling around because people are too lazy to put them in the corrals. I swear, one time I watched a cart slowly roll across three lanes and just slam right into the side of this lady's minivan. Left a massive dent. And she didn't even notice until she came out with her groceries. They really need to hire someone just to round those things up, or maybe do that thing where you have to put a quarter in to get a cart. Aldi does that, and you never see carts loose in their lot. Aldi's weird though, how they make you bag your own stuff on that long counter at the end. And the cashiers sit in chairs while they scan. I don't know why more places don't let their cashiers sit down. Standing on those thin black anti-fatigue mats for eight hours barely does anything for your back. \n\nBut yeah, I know it's a big deal. It's hideous, and I know people are looking at it when they talk to me.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt driven to do certain things, like touching your face, checking your reflection, or adjusting your appearance, because it helps reduce the anxiety or discomfort you feel about how your face looks?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I mean, I have to. It's the only way I can even function, honestly. Like, before I go back out onto the inventory floor at work, I have to stop in the bathroom. I just stand there checking my face in the mirror, trying to fix my cap so it casts a shadow over the left side, and I end up rubbing at that same spot over and over. I get stuck doing it. And sometimes I'm in there so long that I'm late getting back, and my picking numbers fall behind, and my boss gets on me again. \n\nHe's this guy, Dave, who always wears these really tight polo shirts that show off his stomach. He thinks he's this great manager, but all he does is sit in the little glass office overlooking the floor and drink those massive Monster energy drinks. The green ones. He leaves the empty cans lined up on his window sill like some kind of trophy collection. It's gross. And he's always chewing on the plastic stir sticks from the coffee machine. Like, he'll just walk around the warehouse with this mangled piece of plastic hanging out of his mouth, yelling at people about their pick rates. I don't even know why we have those stir sticks anyway, the coffee in the breakroom is basically brown water. It comes out of this machine that looks like it's from the 1980s, and you have to hit the side of it sometimes just to get it to dispense the cup. Half the time, the cup gets jammed and the coffee just pours straight down into the drain grate. \n\nBut yeah, I do the mirror thing constantly. If I don't check it and try to fix it, I just feel this terrible panic building up in my chest. So I have to do it, even if Dave is going to yell at me about my numbers.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself repeating certain behaviors or mental acts—like checking your face in the mirror, touching your face, or thinking about your appearance—because it helps reduce the anxiety or discomfort you feel about how your face looks?\n\nPatient: Wait, didn't you just ask me that? Or something really similar? I feel like we just talked about this. Yeah, I check the mirror and touch my face and all that, like I was just saying. I have to do it to keep the panic down. \n\nIt's kind of like how Dave has to chew on those plastic stir sticks. I bet it's an anxiety thing for him, too, honestly. I mean, the guy is under a lot of pressure from corporate to keep our numbers up. Corporate is always sending down these memos about \"efficiency\" and \"optimizing workflow,\" but they never actually come down to the floor to see how things work. They just look at spreadsheets. One time, this guy from regional came down—wore a full suit to a warehouse, which is just stupid because it's dusty as hell in there. He walked around with this clipboard, nodding at everything, and then two days later they changed the whole layout of aisle four. Made zero sense. We used to have the heavy pet food bags on the bottom racks near the loading docks, which is logical, right? Less distance to move the heavy stuff. But they moved it all the way to the back corner. Now we have to drag those fifty-pound bags of Purina across the entire warehouse. My buddy Mike threw his back out the first week they changed it. He was out for a month on worker's comp, and they fought him on it the whole time. \n\nBut yeah, I do repeat those things. The checking and the touching. I just told you about that.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever have thoughts about your face that come into your mind automatically, that you try to ignore or push away, but they keep coming back no matter how hard you try?\n\nPatient: No, not really. I mean, it's not like the thoughts just pop into my head out of nowhere and I'm trying to fight them off. It's more that I'm just always thinking about it because it's always there. I'm not trying to ignore it, I'm trying to fix it or hide it. \n\nLike I was saying about the cap, I wear it pulled down low. It's a standard issue company hat, navy blue with this ugly orange logo stitched on the front. They make us buy them out of our own paychecks, which I think is illegal, honestly. Ten bucks for a hat that probably cost them fifty cents to make in some sweatshop. And the stitching on the logo always starts fraying after a couple of weeks. You see guys walking around with these long orange threads just dangling off their foreheads. Mike tried to take a lighter to his to burn the loose threads off, but he held it there too long and melted a hole right through the front of the cap. Dave wrote him up for \"destruction of company property\" even though Mike bought the hat himself. Dave's a real piece of work. He actually tried to make Mike buy a new one the very next day, but Mike just wore his old one backwards so the hole was in the back. Dave hated that, but there wasn't a specific rule against wearing it backwards in the handbook. \n\nBut no, I don't get those automatic thoughts you're talking about. I just know it looks terrible, so I'm always aware of it.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel anxious or self-conscious in social situations even when you're not focused on how your face looks?\n\nPatient: I mean, I don't think so. I'm pretty much always focused on how my face looks if I'm around people, so I don't really know what it's like to not be focused on it. But I guess if I'm just hanging out with Mike outside of work, I'm okay. \n\nWe usually just go to this dive bar down the street from the warehouse after our shift. It's called The Rusty Anchor, which is hilarious because we're in Ohio, nowhere near an ocean. The owner, Sal, is this old guy who claims he was in the Navy, but Mike swears he looked him up online once and found out Sal was actually an accountant in Toledo for thirty years. Sal makes these terrible hot wings. Like, they're not even spicy, they're just soaked in vinegar until the meat is basically mush. But they're cheap, and the beer is cold, so we go. Sal has this jukebox in the corner that hasn't been updated since 2005. It's got, like, three different Nickelback albums on it. And there's always this one regular, this guy named Frank, who plays \"Photograph\" at least twice a night. Frank sits at the end of the bar and just stares at the TV, even when it's just playing local news with the sound off. \n\nBut yeah, I don't really get anxious just being around people in general, unless I'm thinking about them staring at my face. Which is, you know, most of the time.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel intense anxiety or fear of being judged about your appearance when you're in public or around people, even if you're not actively thinking about your face at that exact moment?\n\nPatient: I mean, I'm really not sure how to answer that. Like I was just saying, I don't think there's ever a time I'm around people and not actively thinking about my face. It's just always right there in the front of my mind. So I don't really know if I'd be anxious otherwise, because that situation just doesn't happen for me. \n\nIf I'm out in public, like at the grocery store, I'm always on edge about it. I usually go to the Kroger on 5th late at night to try and avoid the crowds. They actually just put in all these new self-checkout machines, the ones with those little cameras that show you on the screen while you scan your stuff. I absolutely hate those. But anyway, the new machines are super glitchy. Almost every time I buy a case of Gatorade for the warehouse, it freezes up and screams about an unexpected item in the bagging area. So then the attendant has to come over and override it. \n\nIt's usually this older lady named Barb working the night shift. Barb is nice, but she talks your ear off. Last time she spent ten minutes telling me about her grandson's travel baseball team while she was trying to fix the machine. Apparently, the parents have to pay for all the hotel rooms out of pocket, and they're driving all the way down to Florida next month for some tournament. I just can't imagine spending that kind of money on a ten-year-old's baseball game. Like, just let the kid play in the local park, you know? Plus, Barb said they make the kids sell those overpriced chocolate bars to fundraise, and nobody even carries cash to buy those anymore. She tried to sell me one right there at the register, which I'm pretty sure is against store policy, but I just bought it anyway so she'd finish typing in her code. \n\nBut yeah, to answer your question, I don't really know. I'm just always thinking about how terrible my face looks whenever I'm in public, so I can't really say what it would feel like if my brain ever took a break from it.\n\nDoctor: How much does the concern about your face interfere with your ability to function normally in your daily life, such as at work, in social settings, or with personal relationships?\n\nPatient: It interferes a lot, honestly. Like, at the warehouse, I've actually been written up recently because my work gets delayed. I'm just so distracted trying to hide my face that I fall behind on my pallets. Dave was the one who wrote me up, of course. He used this official yellow carbon copy paper for the form. I didn't even know they still made carbon copy paper, you know? You have to press down super hard with the pen or the bottom sheet is just completely blank. And Dave uses these cheap ballpoint pens that always skip, so he was basically carving the words into the clipboard trying to get it to copy through. He buys those pens in bulk from some office supply catalog because he thinks it saves the department money, but half of them don't even work straight out of the box. You end up throwing them away, so I don't see how it's saving anything.\n\nBut anyway, yeah, it affects everything else too. I've started skipping pretty much everything outside of work. I used to play in a rec soccer league, and I'd go to these salsa nights downtown, but I just stopped showing up. And family gatherings... I skip those now too. I just can't deal with people looking at me. \n\nAnd the pictures are the absolute worst part. My sister is obsessed with taking pictures at every single family dinner. She just bought this new mirrorless camera with this massive lens that makes her look like she's shooting for National Geographic or something. She spent like two thousand dollars on it, which is insane because she mostly just takes pictures of her cats. She named them Mac and Cheese. It was funny at first, but now she has a whole Instagram for them and makes people follow it. But she always wants these big group photos with the flash on, and I just... I can't have my face in those pictures. I can't have people looking at it. So it's just easier to stay home.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I don't really know how to answer that, honestly. It's hard to say if I feel distressed when I'm not checking it, because... well, I'm pretty much always checking it or trying to fix it. So there isn't really a time where I'm just sitting there not doing it. 

Speaking of checking it, the mirrors in the warehouse bathrooms are completely ruined now. They replaced the glass ones with these cheap metal reflective sheets last month because somebody broke one. Now every time someone tries to heat up their lunch, the microwave makes this terrifying buzzing sound and smells like burning metal. Gary's a nice guy, though. He always saves the extra packets of hot sauce he finds in the breakroom for me. 

But yeah, I don't really know if the anxiety is there when I'm not checking, because I just don't ever really stop checking.

I can't have my face in those pictures. I can't have people looking at it. It's hard to say if I feel distressed when I'm not checking it, because... well, I'm pretty much always checking it or trying to fix it. So there isn't really a time where I'm just sitting there not doing it. \n\nSpeaking of checking it, the mirrors in the warehouse bathrooms are completely ruined now. They replaced the glass ones with these cheap metal reflective sheets last month because somebody broke one. Dave says it's for safety, but I know it's just because the company didn't want to pay for real glass. The metal is all warped, kind of like a funhouse mirror, and it's got these huge scratches all over it from the cleaning crew. They use these heavy-duty green scouring pads on literally everything. I saw the night janitor, Gary, using one on the breakroom microwave once. He completely stripped the paint off the inside of the door. Now every time someone tries to heat up their lunch, the microwave makes this terrifying buzzing sound and smells like burning metal. Gary's a nice guy, though. He always saves the extra packets of hot sauce he finds in the breakroom for me. \n\nBut yeah, I don't really know if the anxiety is there when I'm not checking, because I just don't ever really stop checking."
  }
